2026-02-17 2 use cases 2 techniques 3 kill-chain phases detected

Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).

ATT&CKT1195.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine
// Replace `"setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe"` with the vendor binaries observed in the
// article (matching shape with the Defender query).
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\rundll32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\regsvr32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wmic.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\bitsadmin.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d09
status: experimental
description: |
    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)
    spawning script-host or LOLBin children. Strongest signal when
    paired with a recent CVE / supply-chain advisory naming the same
    vendor.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1195/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1195
    - attack.initial-access
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\wmic.exe'
            - '\bitsadmin.exe'
    selection_parent_vendor:
        ParentImage|contains:
            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'
    condition: selection_child and selection_parent_vendor
falsepositives:
    - Many — installers and updaters legitimately spawn children. Pin
      the parent path to specific vendor binaries observed in the
      article before alerting.
level: medium
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`

// Article-specific bespoke detection — Securing the Agent Skill Ecosystem: How Snyk and Vercel Are Locking Down the New
// Hunts the actual binaries / paths / commandline fragments named
// in the article instead of a generic technique-class template.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where (FileName in~ ("skills.sh"))
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName,
          FolderPath, ProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc

// File-creation events for the named binaries / paths

| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated","FileModified")
| where (FileName in~ ("skills.sh"))
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FolderPath,
          FileName, ActionType, InitiatingProcessFileName,
          InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc

Data sourcesVulnerabilities DeviceInfo DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2020-7796")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where Timestamp > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2020-7796")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

TeamT5 ThreatSonar Anti-Ransomware contains an unrestricted upload of file with dangerous type vulnerability. ThreatSonar Anti-Ransomware does not properly validate the content of uploaded files. Ransomware-style mass file rename / extension change Actions on Objectives Medium

ATT&CKT1486
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem DeviceFileEvents
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| where ActionType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(FileName) by DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, bin(Timestamp, 1m)
| where files > 200    // empirical: > 200 unique-file renames in 1m by one account on one host
                       //            is well above the P99 of legitimate bulk-tooling
| order by files desc

ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| where EventType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(TargetFileName)
            by DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, bin(TimeGenerated, 1m)
| where files > 200    // P99 of legit bulk-tooling per-minute rename volume
| order by files desc
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count, dc(Filesystem.file_name) AS files
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("modified","renamed")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where files > 200
| sort - files
| tstats summariesonly=false count, dc(Filesystem.file_name) AS files
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("modified","renamed")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where files > 200
| sort - files
LSASS process access / dump (credential theft) Actions on Objectives High
Mimikatz, comsvcs.dll MiniDump, or any non-Windows process opening LSASS.
ATT&CKT1003.001 T1003
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceEvents
DeviceEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where ActionType == "OpenProcessApiCall"
| where FileName =~ "lsass.exe"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("MsSense.exe","MsMpEng.exe","csrss.exe",
                                          "svchost.exe","wininit.exe","services.exe",
                                          "lsm.exe","SearchProtocolHost.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFolderPath, AccountName
| order by Timestamp desc
// SecurityEvent EventID 4663 = object access. AccessMask 0x10 / 0x40 / 0x1010
// indicate read access to lsass.exe — credential-dump signature. Audit
// Object Access > Audit File System policy required.
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4663
| where ObjectName endswith "\lsass.exe"
| where Account !endswith "$"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ObjectName, ProcessName,
          AccessMask, AccessReason
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: LSASS Process Access Outside Microsoft-Signed Tooling
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d04
status: experimental
description: |
    Anything opening lsass.exe with read-memory access that isn't a
    Microsoft signed component (Defender, csrss, etc.) is suspect.
    Mimikatz / Pypykatz / nanodump signature.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1003/001/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1003.001
    - attack.credential-access
logsource:
    category: process_access
    product: windows
detection:
    selection:
        TargetImage|endswith: '\lsass.exe'
    filter_known_good:
        SourceImage|endswith:
            - '\MsSense.exe'
            - '\MsMpEng.exe'
            - '\csrss.exe'
            - '\svchost.exe'
            - '\wininit.exe'
            - '\services.exe'
            - '\lsm.exe'
            - '\SearchProtocolHost.exe'
    condition: selection and not filter_known_good
falsepositives:
    - Third-party EDR / forensic tools — extend the filter list
level: high
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where (Processes.process="*lsass*" OR Processes.process="*sekurlsa*"
        OR Processes.process="*MiniDump*" OR Processes.process="*comsvcs.dll*MiniDump*"
        OR Processes.process="*procdump*lsass*")
       OR (Processes.process_name="rundll32.exe" AND Processes.process="*comsvcs*MiniDump*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process_name, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where (Processes.process="*lsass*" OR Processes.process="*sekurlsa*"
        OR Processes.process="*MiniDump*" OR Processes.process="*comsvcs.dll*MiniDump*"
        OR Processes.process="*procdump*lsass*")
       OR (Processes.process_name="rundll32.exe" AND Processes.process="*comsvcs*MiniDump*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process_name, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
Remote service execution — PsExec / SMB lateral movement Actions on Objectives High
psexec / paexec / smbexec / wmic /node — service install over SMB from remote host.
ATT&CKT1021.002 T1569.002

2026-02-17 1 use case 1 technique 2 kill-chain phases detected

Microsoft Windows Video ActiveX Control contains a remote code execution vulnerability. An attacker could exploit the vulnerability by constructing a specially crafted Web page. description: |

            - '\Program Files\'

Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children · Article-specific behavioural hunt — npm backdoor lets hackers hijack gambling outcomes
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] Installation of sidoraress malicious npm packages (json-bigint-extend/jsonfb/jsonfx)
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → [LLM] Egress to sidoraress json-bigint-extend gambling backdoor C2 infrastructure · [LLM] Inbound HTTP request bearing sidoraress backdoor x-operation operator tokens · Network connections to article IPs / domains
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → Crypto-wallet file/keystore access by non-wallet process · Infostealer — non-browser process accessing browser cookie/login DBs
Egress to sidoraress json-bigint-extend gambling backdoor C2 infrastructure AICommand & Control High
Hunts outbound HTTP/DNS traffic from Node.js servers to the five C2 hostnames hard-coded in the json-bigint-extend / jsonfb / jsonfx backdoor (payment.y1pay.vip, payment.snip-site.cc, gameland.21game.live, gameland.myapptest.top, gameland.nbzysp1.com). The backdoor polls /v1/risk/get-risk-code on these hosts every 30 seconds to fetch the gameplay-rewriting payload, so any successful contact is high-fidelity evidence that an infected dependency reached its trigger environment. Rationale: All five hostnames are listed as IOCs by Aikido and verified through the article's narrative description of the polling loop (riskCode refresh every 30s against /v1/risk/get-risk-code on payment.y1pay.vip). The domains are typo-squat / DGA-style infrastructure with no legitimate use — a single hit is a confirmed compromise. The /v1/risk/get-risk-code and /v1/risk/log paths add a second high-fidelity match surface if hostnames are proxied through a CDN. Cross-checked against: • https://www.aikido.dev/blog/npm-backdoor-lets-hackers-hijack-gambling-outcomes • https://github.com/sidorares/json-bigint
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1568.002 T1195.002
Data sourcesWeb.Web Network_Resolution.DNS Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
let _c2_hosts = dynamic(["payment.y1pay.vip","payment.snip-site.cc","gameland.21game.live","gameland.myapptest.top","gameland.nbzysp1.com"]);
let _c2_paths = dynamic(["/v1/risk/get-risk-code","/v1/risk/log"]);
union isfuzzy=true
  (DeviceNetworkEvents
   | where Timestamp > ago(30d)
   | where RemoteUrl has_any (_c2_hosts) or RemoteUrl has_any (_c2_paths)
   | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, RemoteUrl, RemoteIP, RemotePort,
             InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
             InitiatingProcessFolderPath, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessSHA256),
  (DeviceEvents
   | where Timestamp > ago(30d)
   | where ActionType == "DnsQueryResponse"
   | extend QueryName = tostring(parse_json(AdditionalFields).QueryName)
   | where QueryName has_any (_c2_hosts) or QueryName endswith ".y1pay.vip" or QueryName endswith ".snip-site.cc" or QueryName endswith ".21game.live" or QueryName endswith ".myapptest.top" or QueryName endswith ".nbzysp1.com"
   | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, QueryName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessAccountName)
| order by Timestamp desc
let _c2_hosts = dynamic(["payment.y1pay.vip","payment.snip-site.cc","gameland.21game.live","gameland.myapptest.top","gameland.nbzysp1.com"]);
let _c2_path = "/v1/risk/get-risk-code";
union isfuzzy=true
  (CommonSecurityLog
   | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
   | where DestinationHostName has_any (_c2_hosts)
         or RequestURL has_any (_c2_hosts)
         or RequestURL has _c2_path
   | project TimeGenerated, DeviceVendor, DeviceProduct, SourceIP, SourceUserName,
             DestinationIP, DestinationHostName, DestinationPort, RequestURL, RequestMethod, DeviceAction),
  (DnsEvents
   | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
   | where Name has_any (_c2_hosts) or Name endswith ".y1pay.vip" or Name endswith ".snip-site.cc"
         or Name endswith ".21game.live" or Name endswith ".myapptest.top" or Name endswith ".nbzysp1.com"
   | project TimeGenerated, Computer, ClientIP, Name, QueryType, ResultCodeName),
  (ImDnsActivity
   | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
   | where DnsQuery has_any (_c2_hosts) or DnsQuery endswith ".y1pay.vip" or DnsQuery endswith ".snip-site.cc"
         or DnsQuery endswith ".21game.live" or DnsQuery endswith ".myapptest.top" or DnsQuery endswith ".nbzysp1.com"
   | project TimeGenerated, SrcIpAddr, SrcHostname, DnsQuery, DnsQueryTypeName, EventResult)
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Installation of sidoraress malicious npm packages (json-bigint-extend/jsonfb/jsonfx) AIInstallation High
Detects developer, build-server, or CI/CD process events where npm/yarn/pnpm/npx is invoked with one of the three confirmed malicious package names from the sidoraress / infinitynodestudio cluster. Because the backdoor only fires when SERVICE_NAME matches the target gambling platform, install-time detection is the cleanest pre-trigger choke point. Rationale: Package names jsonfb, jsonfx, and json-bigint-extend are listed as IOCs in the Aikido article and confirmed by checking npm — the legitimate maintainer of json-bigint is `sidorares` (single s) while the malicious cluster publishes under `sidoraress` (double s) and `infinitynodestudio`. Pairing the unique package strings with an npm-family tool binary eliminates any chance of incidental string hits. Detection runs install-time, before the SERVICE_NAME-gated payload activates. Cross-checked against: • https://www.aikido.dev/blog/npm-backdoor-lets-hackers-hijack-gambling-outcomes • https://www.npmjs.com/package/json-bigint • https://github.com/sidorares/json-bigint
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1059.007
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
let _pkg_managers = dynamic(["npm.exe","node.exe","yarn.exe","pnpm.exe","npx.exe","npm","node","yarn","pnpm","npx"]);
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where FileName in~ (_pkg_managers) or InitiatingProcessFileName in~ (_pkg_managers)
| where (ProcessCommandLine has_any ("jsonfb","jsonfx") or ProcessCommandLine contains "json-bigint-extend")
     or (InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("jsonfb","jsonfx") or InitiatingProcessCommandLine contains "json-bigint-extend")
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessParentFileName, InitiatingProcessFolderPath
| order by Timestamp desc
let _pkg_suffix = dynamic(["\\npm.exe","\\node.exe","\\yarn.exe","\\pnpm.exe","\\npx.exe"]);
union isfuzzy=true
  (SecurityEvent
   | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
   | where EventID == 4688
   | where Account !endswith "$"
   | where NewProcessName has_any (_pkg_suffix) or ParentProcessName has_any (_pkg_suffix)
   | where CommandLine has_any ("jsonfb","jsonfx") or CommandLine contains "json-bigint-extend"
   | project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName, CommandLine, ParentProcessName),
  (ImProcessCreate
   | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
   | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
   | where TargetProcessName has_any ("npm","node","yarn","pnpm","npx") or ParentProcessName has_any ("npm","node","yarn","pnpm","npx")
   | where TargetProcessCommandLine has_any ("jsonfb","jsonfx") or TargetProcessCommandLine contains "json-bigint-extend" or ParentProcessCommandLine has_any ("jsonfb","jsonfx") or ParentProcessCommandLine contains "json-bigint-extend"
   | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, TargetProcessName, TargetProcessCommandLine, ParentProcessName, ParentProcessCommandLine)
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Inbound HTTP request bearing sidoraress backdoor x-operation operator tokens AICommand & Control High
Detects requests to internal Node.js services carrying the four 20-character secret tokens that gate the json-bigint-extend backdoor's RunSQL / RunFileList / RunFileContent / CompressDownload commands. Because the backdoor monkey-patches express.Route.prototype.post and inspects an x-operation header on every POST, any request bearing one of these tokens is a confirmed operator interaction with a planted backdoor. Rationale: The four 20-character base62 tokens are listed verbatim as IOCs in the Aikido article, one per operator command (RunSQL / RunFileList / RunFileContent / CompressDownload). They are random enough that any appearance in HTTP logs is a confirmed inbound operator request against a backdoored Node.js Express app — there is no legitimate scenario where these strings would appear. Defender XDR has no native HTTP-header telemetry, so defender_kql is intentionally empty; detection lives in web-tier logs. Cross-checked against: • https://www.aikido.dev/blog/npm-backdoor-lets-hackers-hijack-gambling-outcomes
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1505.003 T1005
Data sourcesWeb.Web
let _tokens = dynamic(["cfh2DNITa84qpYQ0tdCz","m3QiEkg8Y1r9LFTI5e4f","Y3SrZjVqWOvKsBdpTCh7","SJQf31UJkZ1f88q9m361"]);
union isfuzzy=true
  (W3CIISLog
   | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
   | where csUriQuery has_any (_tokens) or csUriStem has_any (_tokens) or csUserAgent has_any (_tokens) or csReferer has_any (_tokens)
   | project TimeGenerated, Computer, cIP, csMethod, csUriStem, csUriQuery, csUserAgent, scStatus),
  (CommonSecurityLog
   | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
   | where Message has_any (_tokens) or RequestURL has_any (_tokens) or RequestClientApplication has_any (_tokens)
         or DeviceCustomString1 has_any (_tokens) or DeviceCustomString2 has_any (_tokens) or DeviceCustomString3 has_any (_tokens)
         or DeviceCustomString4 has_any (_tokens) or DeviceCustomString5 has_any (_tokens) or DeviceCustomString6 has_any (_tokens)
   | project TimeGenerated, DeviceVendor, DeviceProduct, SourceIP, DestinationIP, RequestURL, RequestMethod, Message),
  (ImWebSession
   | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
   | where Url has_any (_tokens) or UrlOriginal has_any (_tokens) or HttpRequestHeaderUserAgent has_any (_tokens) or HttpRequestHeaderReferer has_any (_tokens)
   | project TimeGenerated, SrcIpAddr, DstHostname, HttpMethod, Url, HttpStatusCode, HttpRequestHeaderUserAgent),
  (AppServiceHTTPLogs
   | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
   | where CsUriQuery has_any (_tokens) or CsUriStem has_any (_tokens) or UserAgent has_any (_tokens) or Referer has_any (_tokens)
   | project TimeGenerated, CsHost, CIp, CsMethod, CsUriStem, CsUriQuery, ScStatus, UserAgent)
| order by TimeGenerated desc

  search (sourcetype=nginx* OR sourcetype=apache* OR sourcetype=iis* OR sourcetype=*waf* OR sourcetype=*cef* OR tag=web)

  | stats count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime by host src sourcetype uri http_method
]
| convert ctime(firstTime) ctime(lastTime)

Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] s1ngularity Nx postinstall — `gh auth token` spawned by node/npm on CI runner
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → [LLM] CI runner anomalous outbound to raw.githubusercontent.com / gist.githubusercontent.com
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → [LLM] AI CLI weaponized for recon — claude/gemini/q invoked under npm install lineage · [LLM] s1ngularity collection artifact — `/tmp/inventory.txt` written by node/npm on runner · [LLM] GitHub Actions runner — process reads runner worker memory to extract GITHUB_TOKEN
s1ngularity Nx postinstall — `gh auth token` spawned by node/npm on CI runner AIInstallation High
Hunts the s1ngularity Nx malware's signature credential-theft step: the malicious telemetry.js postinstall script spawning the GitHub CLI to extract the runner's GH token. Article-specific because legitimate npm postinstalls almost never invoke `gh auth token`, and this is the exact chain StepSecurity flagged on Aug 2025. Rationale: Article specifically describes the malicious post-install spawning `gh auth token` and StepSecurity's Aug 2025 deep-dive plus the Nx security advisory both name telemetry.js as the dropper. The parent-child constraint (npm/node → gh auth token) is high-fidelity because legitimate CI workflows that invoke gh CLI almost never do so from a postinstall lifecycle hook. Cross-checked against: • https://nx.dev/blog/s1ngularity-postmortem • https://github.com/nrwl/nx/security/advisories/GHSA-cxm3-wv7p-598c • https://thehackernews.com/2025/08/malicious-nx-packages-in-s1ngularity.html • https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/supply-chain-security-alert-popular-nx-build-system-package-compromised-with-data-stealing-malware
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1552.001 T1059.007
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate release workflow that uses `gh auth token` to mint a download URL before publishing artifacts
  • Developer running `gh auth setup-git` interactively that gets shimmed via npm-installed wrapper
  • CI bootstrap step that uses `gh auth status` (token argument absent) — should not trigger because the rule requires both 'auth' AND 'token'
  • Custom internal `nx-release` script that legitimately reads the GH token to push tags
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("nx-release","semantic-release","goreleaser") and SuspectPostInstall == false)
Response runbook
Step 1 — Triage: pull the full process tree on the runner for the 5 min surrounding the alert; confirm the npm/node parent and inspect whether the parent commandline references a `postinstall` lifecycle or known compromised package (e.g. `@nx/*`, `nx`, `@nrwl/*`). Pivot on the GH token's owner via `gh api user` from your IR sandbox to see what scopes it had. Step 2 — Contain: revoke any GITHUB_TOKEN, npm token, or PAT minted on that runner, rotate org secrets exposed to that workflow, kill the runner pool host and re-image. Block outbound to `api.github.com` from the runner subnet pending review. Step 3 — Hunt: search the same week for `/tmp/inventory.txt` writes, repos under affected users renamed to `s1ngularity-repository*`, and outbound to raw.githubusercontent.com / gist.githubusercontent.com from any runner. Audit `package-lock.json` diffs for new Nx / @nrwl additions in the suspect window.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("node","npm","npx","yarn","pnpm")
| where FileName =~ "gh"
| where ProcessCommandLine has "auth" and ProcessCommandLine has "token"
| extend SuspectPostInstall = InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("postinstall","telemetry.js","nx/","/@nx/","node_modules")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, SuspectPostInstall, InitiatingProcessParentFileName
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("node","npm","npx","yarn","pnpm")
| where ActingProcessName endswith "/gh" or ActingProcessName =~ "gh"
| where ActingProcessCommandLine has "auth" and ActingProcessCommandLine has "token"
| extend SuspectPostInstall = ParentProcessCommandLine has_any ("postinstall","telemetry.js","node_modules","@nx/")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ParentProcessName, ParentProcessCommandLine, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, SuspectPostInstall
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: GitHub CLI Token Extraction Spawned by Node Package Manager Postinstall

AI CLI weaponized for recon — claude/gemini/q invoked under npm install lineage AIActions on Objectives High
Hunts the novel s1ngularity tradecraft of dropping a prompt that hijacks local AI coding agents (claude, gemini, q) into walking the filesystem and exfiltrating secrets. Article-specific: this is the first publicly documented supply-chain attack that uses installed AI CLIs as the recon engine. Rationale: StepSecurity, Snyk, and Semgrep all corroborate that the malicious postinstall script literally launched `claude --dangerously-skip-permissions`, `gemini --yolo` and `q chat --trust-all-tools` to drive recon — those flag strings are unique to LLM CLIs and never legitimately appear inside an npm lifecycle. The parent-process anchor (any package-manager runtime) keeps FPs tiny because developers run the AI CLI interactively, not as a child of an install. Cross-checked against: • https://snyk.io/blog/weaponizing-ai-coding-agents-for-malware-in-the-nx-malicious-package/ • https://semgrep.dev/blog/2025/security-alert-nx-compromised-to-steal-wallets-and-credentials/ • https://nx.dev/blog/s1ngularity-postmortem
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1059 T1083
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Internal release tooling that wraps Claude Code inside an npm script for code generation
  • Developer running `npm run claude` task that invokes the CLI as part of an authoring workflow (uncommon)
  • Monorepo bootstrap script that delegates to `node ./tools/ai-codegen.js` which shells to gemini
  • CI workflow that uses `q chat` to summarize PR diffs (would normally not pass --trust-all-tools)
Suppression filter
| where not (ParentProcessCommandLine has "ai-codegen" or ParentProcessCommandLine has "internal/release-pipeline")
Response runbook
Step 1 — Triage: capture the parent process's source file (telemetry.js? install.js?) and SHA256 it; check VirusTotal and search internal artifact registry for that hash. Confirm which packages were resolved on this install by reading the runner's `package-lock.json` snapshot. Step 2 — Contain: kill the runner, rotate every credential the AI CLI could have read on disk (~/.aws/credentials, ~/.npmrc, ~/.ssh/, ~/.gnupg, ~/.config/gh, env vars dumped to /tmp), and quarantine the offending package version in your registry mirror. Step 3 — Hunt: across the org, search for any process child of node/npm/yarn invoking the three AI CLIs in the last 90 days, and grep the runner image for `dangerously-skip-permissions` strings in package source. Notify devs to rotate their personal GH tokens.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(14d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("node","npm","npx","yarn","pnpm","sh","bash")
| where FileName in~ ("claude","gemini","q")
   or ProcessCommandLine has_any ("claude --dangerously-skip-permissions","gemini --yolo","q chat --trust-all-tools")
| extend InstallContext = InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("postinstall","telemetry.js","node_modules","@nx/","npm install","npm ci","yarn install","pnpm install")
| where InstallContext
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, FileName, ProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(14d)
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("node","npm","npx","yarn","pnpm","sh","bash")
| where ActingProcessName endswith "/claude" or ActingProcessName endswith "/gemini" or ActingProcessName endswith "/q"
   or ActingProcessCommandLine has_any ("claude --dangerously-skip-permissions","gemini --yolo","q chat --trust-all-tools")
| extend InstallContext = ParentProcessCommandLine has_any ("postinstall","telemetry.js","node_modules","@nx/","npm install","npm ci","yarn install","pnpm install")
| where InstallContext
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ParentProcessName, ParentProcessCommandLine, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

CI runner anomalous outbound to raw.githubusercontent.com / gist.githubusercontent.com AICommand & Control Medium
Hunts the network behaviour that StepSecurity flagged across Flank, Microsoft Azure Karpenter, and tj-actions/changed-files — a CI runner reaching out to raw.githubusercontent.com or gist.githubusercontent.com to pull attacker-controlled code or stage exfil. First-time-seen-per-runner-pool gating turns this into an alertable signal. Rationale: The article quotes the Flank researcher directly: 'picked up an anomalous curl to raw.githubusercontent.com despite some initial effort I made to blend in.' StepSecurity's tj-actions write-up names gist.githubusercontent.com as the smoking-gun anomaly endpoint and the Karpenter incident report names a GitHub gist as the staging origin — three independent campaigns hit the same two hostnames, making the first-seen-per-runner pivot high signal. Baselining excludes the legit case where pipelines pull pinned raw URLs every run. Cross-checked against: • https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/harden-runner-detection-tj-actions-changed-files-action-is-compromised • https://www.wiz.io/blog/github-action-tj-actions-changed-files-supply-chain-attack-cve-2025-30066 • https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/alerts/2025/03/18/supply-chain-compromise-third-party-tj-actionschanged-files-cve-2025-30066-and-reviewdogaction
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1102 T1567
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic Web.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Developer adds a new dependency that fetches install scripts from raw.githubusercontent.com (Homebrew-style installers)
  • Action that uses gist.githubusercontent.com to retrieve a pinned configuration file for the first time
  • Renovate/Dependabot run that introduces a new external Action whose setup step pulls raw.githubusercontent.com
  • Self-hosted runner pool freshly imaged — every URL looks new for the first 24h
Response runbook
Step 1 — Triage: inspect the full URL — pin the specific repo/gist owner — and see whether the gist/raw URL belongs to a known author for your org. Look up the parent workflow that triggered the call via the runner's `_diag/Worker_*.log`. Step 2 — Contain: if the URL points to an unknown author OR the workflow that called it pulls a third-party Action (especially one named in tj-actions/reviewdog/Nx advisories), pause the runner pool and revoke any GITHUB_TOKEN issued in the same workflow run; pin the offending Action to a SHA. Step 3 — Hunt: pivot to the same SHA's parent process — was it a curl/wget invoked inline, or is it a transitive postinstall? Search the prior 24h for the same RemoteUrl across the runner pool and check for downstream calls to webhook.site, requestbin, transfer.sh, or attacker-owned domains.
let LookbackBaseline = 30d;
let Window = 1h;
let RunnerPattern = dynamic(["runner","gh-runner","actions-runner","ghr-"]);
let Baseline = DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp between (ago(LookbackBaseline) .. ago(Window))
| where RemoteUrl has_any ("raw.githubusercontent.com","gist.githubusercontent.com")
| where DeviceName has_any (RunnerPattern)
| summarize by DeviceName, RemoteUrl;
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(Window)
| where RemoteUrl has_any ("raw.githubusercontent.com","gist.githubusercontent.com")
| where DeviceName has_any (RunnerPattern)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("curl","wget","node","python","python3","bash","sh","go")
| join kind=leftanti Baseline on DeviceName, RemoteUrl
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, RemoteUrl, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| order by Timestamp desc
let LookbackBaseline = 30d;
let Window = 1h;
let RunnerPattern = dynamic(["runner","gh-runner","actions-runner","ghr-"]);
let Baseline = ImWebSession
| where TimeGenerated between (ago(LookbackBaseline) .. ago(Window))
| where Url has_any ("raw.githubusercontent.com","gist.githubusercontent.com")
| where DvcHostname has_any (RunnerPattern)
| summarize by DvcHostname, HttpHostName;
ImWebSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(Window)
| where Url has_any ("raw.githubusercontent.com","gist.githubusercontent.com")
| where DvcHostname has_any (RunnerPattern)
| join kind=leftanti Baseline on DvcHostname, HttpHostName
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, SrcUsername, Url, HttpRequestHeaderUserAgent, DstIpAddr
| order by TimeGenerated desc

  }, field=[aid, ContextProcessId], include=[ImageFileName, CommandLine])
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, DomainName, ImageFileName, CommandLine], function=count())

s1ngularity collection artifact — `/tmp/inventory.txt` written by node/npm on runner AIActions on Objectives High
File-event detection for the s1ngularity malware's signature staging file. The malicious postinstall enumerates secrets and writes them to `/tmp/inventory.txt` before base64-encoding for exfil; this is the cleanest single-event IOC for the family and lets you find historical compromises in DeviceFileEvents. Rationale: Kodem, Snyk, and the Nx postmortem all explicitly name `/tmp/inventory.txt` as the survival check (`if this file exists you have been affected`) and `results.b64` as the triple-base64 exfil payload. Pairing the unique filenames with a package-manager process parent locks this to s1ngularity behaviour rather than the dozens of legitimate `inventory.txt` files devs leave around. Cross-checked against: • https://www.kodemsecurity.com/resources/vulnerability-alert-s1ngularity-malicious-nx-npm-packages • https://nx.dev/blog/s1ngularity-postmortem • https://hackmag.com/news/s1ngularity
ATT&CKT1074.001 T1005 T1195.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A `node` build step legitimately writing an inventory.txt manifest (rare, but enterprise monorepos sometimes do)
  • Dev workstation where someone ran a known-good inventory tool out of /tmp during testing
  • Test fixtures named `results.b64` in a node test runner (e.g. unit tests for a base64 encoder)
  • Package documentation example that writes inventory.txt to demo file ops
Response runbook
Step 1 — Triage: hash the file, examine its first 256 bytes (should be NUL-padded text content of secrets if it's the live IOC), and inspect the node process tree that created it. Pull the parent npm/yarn invocation and its working directory to identify the package that ran the postinstall. Step 2 — Contain: snapshot /tmp, then preserve and delete inventory.txt + any results.b64, quarantine the host, force-rotate every credential type the malware exfils (GH PATs, npm tokens, AWS/Azure/GCP keys in env or files, SSH keys, wallet seeds). Lock the GH user's repos to prevent the rename-to-public step. Step 3 — Hunt: search across all engineering laptops + runners for /tmp/inventory.txt writes in the prior 90 days, the GH org for any repo named `s1ngularity-repository*` (and private→public flips), and npm logs for installs of `nx`, `@nrwl/*`, or `@nx/*` in the affected timeframe.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated","FileModified")
| where (FolderPath =~ "/tmp" and FileName =~ "inventory.txt")
   or FileName endswith ".b64" and FileName has "results"
   or FolderPath has "/tmp" and FileName =~ "results.b64"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("node","npm","npx","yarn","pnpm","sh","bash")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, FolderPath, FileName, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where EventType in ("FileCreated","FileModified")
| where (TargetFilePath =~ "/tmp/inventory.txt")
   or (TargetFileName =~ "inventory.txt" and TargetFilePath startswith "/tmp")
   or (TargetFileName =~ "results.b64")
| where ActingProcessName has_any ("node","npm","npx","yarn","pnpm","sh","bash")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256
| order by TimeGenerated desc

GitHub Actions runner — process reads runner worker memory to extract GITHUB_TOKEN AIActions on Objectives Medium
Hunts the tj-actions/changed-files tradecraft (also reused in Flank PoC): a Python or sidecar process opening `/proc/<runner-worker-pid>/maps` and `/proc/<pid>/mem` on a CI runner to scrape the in-memory GITHUB_TOKEN. Article-specific because StepSecurity attributes both incidents to this exact memory-read pattern. Rationale: CISA, Wiz, and StepSecurity's own tj-actions write-up describe the malicious Python script as reading Runner.Worker memory via the /proc filesystem to extract GITHUB_TOKEN — Flank reused the same memory-read trick. /proc/<pid>/mem reads from non-root non-system-debug processes are intrinsically rare, especially gated to runner-named hosts. Cross-checked against: • https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/harden-runner-detection-tj-actions-changed-files-action-is-compromised • https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/alerts/2025/03/18/supply-chain-compromise-third-party-tj-actionschanged-files-cve-2025-30066-and-reviewdogaction • https://www.wiz.io/blog/github-action-tj-actions-changed-files-supply-chain-attack-cve-2025-30066
ATT&CKT1003.007 T1552.006 T1059.006
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate observability agent (e.g. Datadog APM, Pyroscope) reading /proc/<pid>/maps for profile sampling
  • Container runtime tooling (crun, containerd-shim) walking /proc
  • Custom debug script that an SRE runs to diagnose a hung pipeline
  • gcore / gdb on the runner during an incident postmortem
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("datadog-agent","node_exporter","pyroscope","containerd-shim") or InitiatingProcessAccountName in~ ("root","systemd-coredump"))
Response runbook
Step 1 — Triage: capture the reading process's full commandline + its parent — was it launched from a third-party Action (`uses: tj-actions/changed-files@…`, `uses: reviewdog/…`)? Cross-reference the Action pin to the CVE-2025-30066/CVE-2025-30154 affected ranges. Step 2 — Contain: invalidate the GITHUB_TOKEN issued to the affected workflow run via the GitHub API, rotate any secrets the workflow had access to, and pin every third-party Action in your org to a full commit SHA (not a tag) immediately. Step 3 — Hunt: search the prior 90 days for any /proc/*/mem read by a non-root non-systemd process across the entire runner fleet, then pivot on the parent workflow names and any outbound traffic to gist.githubusercontent.com or attacker-staged exfil endpoints in the same workflow run.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated","FileModified","FileAccessed")
| where FolderPath startswith "/proc/" and (FileName in ("mem","maps","environ"))
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("python","python3","node","bash","sh","curl","wget","go")
| where DeviceName has_any ("runner","gh-runner","actions-runner","ghr-")
| extend WorkerHint = InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("Runner.Worker","actions-runner","_diag")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, FolderPath, FileName, WorkerHint
| order by Timestamp desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where TargetFilePath startswith "/proc/" and (TargetFileName in ("mem","maps","environ"))
| where ActingProcessName has_any ("python","python3","node","bash","sh","curl","wget","go")
| where DvcHostname has_any ("runner","gh-runner","actions-runner","ghr-")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, TargetFilePath
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: Process Reads Proc Filesystem Memory on GitHub Actions Runner
id: 5c1f7b2a-9e88-4b46-bc9e-7e9d6c3f201e
description: Detects a Python, Node, or shell process accessing /proc/<pid>/mem or /proc/<pid>/maps on a host whose name matches a GitHub Actions runner pattern — the tj-actions/changed-files compromise extracted GITHUB_TOKEN from the Runner.Worker process via this exact read.
references:

Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children Exploitation Medium
Supply-chain trojan signal: legitimate signed binary spawning script interpreters or exotic LOLBins.
ATT&CKT1195.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine
// Replace `"setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe"` with the vendor binaries observed in the
// article (matching shape with the Defender query).
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\rundll32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\regsvr32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wmic.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\bitsadmin.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d09
status: experimental
description: |
    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)
    spawning script-host or LOLBin children. Strongest signal when
    paired with a recent CVE / supply-chain advisory naming the same

    category: process_creation

            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\cmd.exe'

            - '\regsvr32.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\wmic.exe'
            - '\bitsadmin.exe'

        ParentImage|contains:
            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'

      the parent path to specific vendor binaries observed in the
      article before alerting.

20+ Popular NPM Packages Compromised (Chalk, Debug, Strip-ANSI, Color-Convert, Wrap-ANSI...)

StepSecurity
2026-02-15 10 use cases 5 techniques 6 kill-chain phases detected

Massive NPM supply chain attack targets cryptocurrency users through compromised maintainer account - affecting packages downloaded billions of times weekly including debug, chalk, ansi-styles, color-convert, strip-ansi and 15+ other critical JavaScript packages. Malicious code injected to steal cryptocurrency wallets and redirect blockchain transactions.

Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → [LLM] Phishing email impersonating npm support from typosquatted npmjs.help domain · Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution · Email attachment opened from external sender
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Office app spawning script/LOLBin child process · Fake CAPTCHA / clipboard-injected PowerShell (ClickFix / FakeCaptcha) · Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] Install of Qix-compromised npm package@version (chalk 5.6.1, debug 4.4.2, ansi-styles 6.2.2 et al.)
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → [LLM] Egress to Qix npm phishing/exfil infrastructure (npmjs.help, publicvm.com, BunnyCDN buckets)
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → Crypto-wallet file/keystore access by non-wallet process · Infostealer — non-browser process accessing browser cookie/login DBs
Phishing email impersonating npm support from typosquatted npmjs.help domain AIDelivery High
Hunts inbound mail from the attacker's typosquatted lookalike domain npmjs.help used to spoof an NPM 2FA reset and harvest maintainer credentials/TOTP in the Qix account takeover (Sept 8 2025). Single sender-domain pivot — the legitimate domain is npmjs.com, so any traffic from npmjs.help is unambiguously malicious. Rationale: Sender-domain match on `npmjs.help` is unambiguous — the only legitimate npm domain is npmjs.com (and npmjs.org). External corroboration via Aikido, Wiz, Upwind, Semgrep, Cycode all confirm npmjs.help as the phishing source and 185.7.81.108 as its hosting IP. Sentinel-side left empty: the EmailEvents/EmailUrlInfo tables are Defender-XDR-native and have no clean Sentinel equivalent in the schema available (OfficeActivity does not surface inbound mail metadata at this fidelity). Datadog and Falcon LogScale lack standard inbound-email telemetry sources in this attack's relevant data feed, so both are left empty. Cross-checked against: • https://www.aikido.dev/blog/npm-debug-and-chalk-packages-compromised • https://www.upwind.io/feed/npm-supply-chain-attack-massive-compromise-of-debug-chalk-and-16-other-packages • https://www.wiz.io/blog/widespread-npm-supply-chain-attack-breaking-down-impact-scope-across-debug-chalk • https://semgrep.dev/blog/2025/chalk-debug-and-color-on-npm-compromised-in-new-supply-chain-attack/
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1656
Data sourcesEmail
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Internal red-team exercise replaying the npmjs.help lure for training
  • Threat-intel mailing list forwarding a screenshot/quoted-source that includes npmjs.help in the body but not in headers (won't trigger this rule which is sender-side)
  • Legitimate domain reuse — extremely unlikely as the domain was used purely for phishing and was sinkholed/taken down
Response runbook
Step 1 — Triage: pull the recipient list, confirm whether any maintainer or DevOps engineer received the lure and whether they clicked (join to UrlClickEvents on NetworkMessageId). Step 2 — Contain: invoke an emergency npm-credential rotation (npm publish tokens, ~/.npmrc tokens, CI/CD npm secrets) and a hardware-key 2FA enforcement for anyone who interacted. Run `npm token list` against every account and revoke any unrecognised tokens. Step 3 — Hunt: pivot to UC2 (compromised package@version present in environment) and UC3 (DNS/network egress to attacker infra) for the same recipient's devices and CI runners over the next 24h.
// Qix npm phishing — sender domain npmjs.help (legitimate domain is npmjs.com)
let LookbackDays = 30d;
let PhishDomains = dynamic(["npmjs.help"]);
let MailHits =
    EmailEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where SenderMailFromDomain in~ (PhishDomains)
       or SenderFromDomain in~ (PhishDomains)
       or SenderMailFromAddress endswith "@npmjs.help"
       or SenderFromAddress endswith "@npmjs.help"
    | project Timestamp, NetworkMessageId, RecipientEmailAddress,
              SenderMailFromAddress, SenderFromAddress, Subject,
              DeliveryAction, DeliveryLocation, ThreatTypes, UrlCount;
let UrlHits =
    EmailUrlInfo
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where Url has "npmjs.help"
    | project Timestamp, NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain;
MailHits
| join kind=leftouter UrlHits on NetworkMessageId
| project Timestamp, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderMailFromAddress,
          SenderFromAddress, Subject, DeliveryAction, DeliveryLocation,
          ThreatTypes, Url, NetworkMessageId
| order by Timestamp desc

Install of Qix-compromised npm package@version (chalk 5.6.1, debug 4.4.2, ansi-styles 6.2.2 et al.) AIInstallation High
Hunts for `npm install`, `yarn add`, or `pnpm add` of the exact package@version strings published from the hijacked Qix maintainer account on Sept 8 2025. Also catches the post-install footprint (the malicious version landing inside node_modules) via file-event pivot. Rationale: Anchored on the 19 exact package@version strings confirmed across StepSecurity, Aikido, Semgrep, and Wiz — these versions only existed on the registry during a ~2.5h window on Sept 8 2025, so any install/cmd-line reference is high-fidelity. Pivots on both process-creation (the install command) and file-event (resulting node_modules/package-lock.json write) to catch hands-off CI/CD installs where the developer didn't manually type the version. Cross-checked against: • https://www.aikido.dev/blog/npm-debug-and-chalk-packages-compromised • https://semgrep.dev/blog/2025/chalk-debug-and-color-on-npm-compromised-in-new-supply-chain-attack/ • https://www.upwind.io/feed/npm-supply-chain-attack-massive-compromise-of-debug-chalk-and-16-other-packages • https://www.wiz.io/blog/widespread-npm-supply-chain-attack-breaking-down-impact-scope-across-debug-chalk
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1059.007
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes Endpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Internal package-lock.json analysis tools enumerating these names to check exposure (legitimate hunting activity by your own security team)
  • Researcher / red-teamer downloading the malicious tarballs into a sandbox for analysis
  • Mirrored / cached registry serving a stale copy of one of these versions to an air-gapped CI runner after upstream cleanup
  • Open-source TI script that prints the IOC list into a log file that Defender then scoops as cmdline
Suppression filter
// Optional exclusion of internal TI-enumeration tooling and known sandbox hosts
| where DeviceName !in~ ("SOC-SANDBOX-01","MALANALYSIS-VM")
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe") or InitiatingProcessCommandLine !has "Get-NPMCompromiseExposure"
Response runbook
Step 1 — Triage: confirm whether the device/CI runner actually executed `npm install` (vs. just having the IOC string in a TI script) by checking child processes of the npm.exe parent and whether node_modules/<pkg>/package.json was written with the malicious version. Step 2 — Contain: if confirmed install, isolate the host/runner, kill any node.exe processes, snapshot node_modules, rotate any secrets the build process had access to (CI tokens, npm publish tokens, AWS/GCP creds in env vars). Step 3 — Hunt: pivot to UC3 (egress to npmjs.help / publicvm.com / b-cdn.net), check whether any developer-machine browsers loaded a bundled artifact containing these packages, and audit `git log` for package-lock.json changes around Sept 8 2025 13:00-16:00 UTC.
// Qix npm compromise — install of one of the 19 malicious package@version pairs
let LookbackDays = 60d;
let BadVersions = dynamic([
    "chalk@5.6.1", "debug@4.4.2", "ansi-styles@6.2.2",
    "ansi-regex@6.2.1", "strip-ansi@7.1.1", "color-convert@3.1.1",
    "color-name@2.0.1", "color@5.0.1", "color-string@2.1.1",
    "supports-color@10.2.1", "wrap-ansi@9.0.1", "slice-ansi@7.1.1",
    "is-arrayish@0.3.3", "simple-swizzle@0.2.3", "supports-hyperlinks@4.1.1",
    "has-ansi@6.0.1", "chalk-template@1.1.1", "backslash@0.2.1",
    "error-ex@1.3.3"
]);
let CmdHits =
    DeviceProcessEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where FileName in~ ("npm.exe","npm.cmd","yarn.exe","yarn.cmd","pnpm.exe","pnpm.cmd","node.exe")
         or InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("npm.exe","yarn.exe","pnpm.exe","node.exe")
    | where ProcessCommandLine has_any (BadVersions)
         or InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any (BadVersions)
    | project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName,
              FileName, ProcessCommandLine,
              ParentName = InitiatingProcessFileName,
              ParentCmd  = InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
              Source = "cmdline";
let FileHits =
    DeviceFileEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where ActionType in~ ("FileCreated","FileModified")
    | where (FolderPath has @"\node_modules\chalk\"     and FileName =~ "package.json")
         or (FolderPath has @"\node_modules\debug\"     and FileName =~ "package.json")
         or (FolderPath has @"\node_modules\ansi-styles\" and FileName =~ "package.json")
         or (FolderPath has @"\node_modules\color\"     and FileName =~ "package.json")
         or (FileName =~ "package-lock.json")
    | project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName,
              FileName = strcat(FolderPath, "\\", FileName), ProcessCommandLine = InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
              ParentName = InitiatingProcessFileName, ParentCmd = InitiatingProcessParentFileName,
              Source = "file";
union CmdHits, FileHits
| order by Timestamp desc

Egress to Qix npm phishing/exfil infrastructure (npmjs.help, publicvm.com, BunnyCDN buckets) AICommand & Control Medium
Hunts DNS resolution or outbound network connections to the four corroborated attacker-controlled hosts in the Sept 8 2025 npm compromise: the phishing landing page (npmjs.help / 185.7.81.108), the credential-stealer C2 (websocket-api2.publicvm.com), and the two BunnyCDN payload buckets (static-mw-host.b-cdn.net, img-data-backup.b-cdn.net). All four are confirmed across Aikido, Upwind, Wiz, and CSO Online. Rationale: Five concrete network IOCs corroborated across Aikido (original detector), Upwind, Wiz, CSO Online and Phoenix Security: domain `npmjs.help` (phishing lure host), IP `185.7.81.108` (its A record), `websocket-api2.publicvm.com` (credential exfil), and the BunnyCDN buckets `static-mw-host.b-cdn.net` / `img-data-backup.b-cdn.net` (payload hosting). All four hostnames are non-overlapping with legitimate npm infrastructure (the real domain is npmjs.com), making any hit unambiguously malicious. Cross-checked against: • https://www.aikido.dev/blog/npm-debug-and-chalk-packages-compromised • https://www.upwind.io/feed/npm-supply-chain-attack-massive-compromise-of-debug-chalk-and-16-other-packages • https://www.wiz.io/blog/widespread-npm-supply-chain-attack-breaking-down-impact-scope-across-debug-chalk • https://www.csoonline.com/article/4053725/massive-npm-supply-chain-attack-hits-18-popular-packages-with-2b-weekly-downloads.html • https://phoenix.security/qix-npm-compromise/
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1568 T1567
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic Network_Resolution.DNS
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • SOC analyst pivoting on these IOCs via a sandbox VM (browser-based investigation of npmjs.help screenshot)
  • TI feed cron job resolving the domains for enrichment without intent to connect — would show DNS but no follow-up TCP
  • Customer running their own copy of publicvm.com (unlikely — this is a free dynamic-DNS service abused by the attacker, the subdomain is attacker-specific)
  • BunnyCDN parent-domain hits from unrelated CDN customers — filter ensures only the specific buckets fire
Suppression filter
// Suppress SOC sandbox + threat-intel enrichment hosts
| where DeviceName !in~ ("SOC-SANDBOX-01","TI-ENRICH-01","MALANALYSIS-VM")
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("thor.exe","yara.exe","misp-agent.exe")
Response runbook
Step 1 — Triage: identify the initiating process for the egress (InitiatingProcessFileName + ProcessCommandLine on Defender). If it's a browser, check whether the user clicked a phishing link from UC1; if it's node/npm, the host installed a compromised package per UC2. Step 2 — Contain: isolate the host, force-rotate all developer credentials in scope (npm tokens, GitHub PATs, cloud SDK creds in ~/.npmrc, ~/.aws/credentials, env). Block all four hostnames + the IP at egress proxy/firewall. Step 3 — Hunt: extract all unique source devices that resolved or connected, pivot to BrowserExtension/clipboard data, and review crypto-wallet transactions originating from any user device for unexpected destination addresses over the last 7 days (the malware silently rewrites destination addresses in the browser).
// Qix npm compromise — egress to phishing/exfil infrastructure
let LookbackDays = 60d;
let BadHosts = dynamic([
    "npmjs.help",
    "websocket-api2.publicvm.com",
    "static-mw-host.b-cdn.net",
    "img-data-backup.b-cdn.net"
]);
let BadIPs = dynamic(["185.7.81.108"]);
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where RemoteUrl has_any (BadHosts)
     or RemoteIP in (BadIPs)
| project Timestamp, DeviceName,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessAccountUpn,
          RemoteUrl, RemoteIP, RemotePort, ActionType
| order by Timestamp desc
// Qix npm compromise — egress to phishing/exfil infrastructure
let LookbackDays = 60d;
let BadHosts = dynamic([
    "npmjs.help",
    "websocket-api2.publicvm.com",
    "static-mw-host.b-cdn.net",
    "img-data-backup.b-cdn.net"
]);
let BadIPs = dynamic(["185.7.81.108"]);
let DnsHits =
    DnsEvents
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where Name in~ (BadHosts) or Name endswith ".npmjs.help"
    | project TimeGenerated, Computer, ClientIP, Name, QueryType, Source = "DnsEvents";
let NetHits =
    CommonSecurityLog
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where DestinationHostName in~ (BadHosts)
         or RequestURL has_any (BadHosts)
         or DestinationIP in (BadIPs)
    | project TimeGenerated, Computer = SourceHostName, ClientIP = SourceIP,
              Name = coalesce(DestinationHostName, RequestURL),
              QueryType = ApplicationProtocol, Source = "CommonSecurityLog";
let WebHits =
    ImWebSession
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where Url has_any (BadHosts) or DstHostname in~ (BadHosts) or DstIpAddr in (BadIPs)
    | project TimeGenerated, Computer = DvcHostname, ClientIP = SrcIpAddr,
              Name = coalesce(DstHostname, Url), QueryType = HttpMethod, Source = "ImWebSession";
union DnsHits, NetHits, WebHits
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc
WEB
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats summariesonly=false count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time

ATT&CKT1566.001 T1204.002

              AttachmentFileName = FileName, AttachmentSHA256 = SHA256;

              Recipients, Subject, MessageId;
SecurityEvent

   or NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"

          ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: FakeCaptcha / ClickFix - Clipboard-Injected PowerShell from Explorer
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d06
status: experimental
description: |
    "FakeCaptcha" / "ClickFix" social-engineering: malicious site asks
    user to press Win+R and paste a payload. The result is
    powershell/mshta launched directly from explorer.exe with iex /
    base64 / downloadstring in the command line.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1059/001/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1204/002/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1059.001
    - attack.t1204.002
    - attack.execution
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_parent:
        ParentImage|endswith:
            - '\explorer.exe'
            - '\RuntimeBroker.exe'
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
    selection_cmd:
        CommandLine|contains:
            - 'iex'
            - 'invoke-expression'
            - 'frombase64'
            - 'downloadstring'
            - 'hxxp'
            - 'curl '
            - 'wget '
    condition: selection_parent and selection_child and selection_cmd
falsepositives:
    - IT-driven scripted automation that admins paste-launch
level: high
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("explorer.exe","RuntimeBroker.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","mshta.exe")
      AND (Processes.process="*iex*" OR Processes.process="*Invoke-Expression*"
        OR Processes.process="*FromBase64*" OR Processes.process="*DownloadString*"
        OR Processes.process="*hxxp*" OR Processes.process="*curl*" OR Processes.process="*wget*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("explorer.exe","RuntimeBroker.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","mshta.exe")
      AND (Processes.process="*iex*" OR Processes.process="*Invoke-Expression*"
        OR Processes.process="*FromBase64*" OR Processes.process="*DownloadString*"
        OR Processes.process="*hxxp*" OR Processes.process="*curl*" OR Processes.process="*wget*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`

CVEsCVE-2024-3094
ATT&CKT1190 T1195.002
SHA256319feb5a9cddd819… 605861f833fc181c… 8fa641c454c3e0f7… b418bfd34aa246b2… cbeef92e67bf41ca…

Detected
Phase 7
Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s) Reconnaissance High
Match vulnerability scanner output to the CVE(s) named in the article.
ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesVulnerabilities DeviceInfo DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2024-3094")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where Timestamp > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2024-3094")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match Installation High
Match SHA256/SHA1/MD5 named in the article against EDR file/process telemetry.
ATT&CKT1027
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Processes DeviceFileEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Pre-filter each table BEFORE the union — the engine cannot push
// predicates across `union`, and DeviceFileEvents lacks ProcessCommandLine
// (Initiating only) so column-projection has to differ per side.
union
    ( DeviceFileEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)

// Pre-filter both ASIM tables before the union — same pattern as the
// Defender version so the engine can push hash predicates per-side.
let BadHashes = dynamic(["319feb5a9cddd81955d915b5632b4a5f8f9080281fb46e2f6d69d53f693c23ae", "605861f833fc181c7cdcabd5577ddb8989bea332648a8f498b4eef89b8f85ad4", "8fa641c454c3e0f76de73b7cc3446096b9c8b9d33d406d38b8ac76090b0344fd", "b418bfd34aa246b2e7b5cb5d263a640e5d080810f767370c4d2c24662a274963", "cbeef92e67bf41ca9c015557d81f39adaba67ca9fb3574139754999030b83537", "5448850cdc3a7ae41ff53b433c2adbd0ff492515012412ee63a40d2685db3049"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc
