APT28 MacroMaze: Edge launched off-screen or headless to webhook.site by non-browser parent AICommand & Control High
Hunts the MacroMaze Stage-2 batch behaviour where msedge.exe is launched by cmd.exe/wscript.exe with the article's specific cloaking flags (--window-size=1,1 --window-position=10000,10000, --headless=new, or --ignore-certificate-errors) and navigates to a webhook[.]site URL used as both payload broker and exfil endpoint. The off-screen positioning combined with webhook.site is a near-deterministic indicator of this campaign. Rationale: Uses the article's verbatim Edge cloaking flags (--window-position=10000,10000, --window-size=1,1, --headless=new, --ignore-certificate-errors) plus the webhook.site C2/exfil host. The combination of msedge.exe spawned by cmd.exe/wscript.exe with these specific flags is essentially unique to MacroMaze. Cross-checked against: • https://thehackernews.com/2026/02/apt28-targeted-european-entities-using.html • https://securityaffairs.com/188421/apt/operation-macromaze-apt28-exploits-webhooks-for-covert-data-exfiltration.html • https://socprime.com/active-threats/operation-macromaze/ • https://attack.mitre.org/groups/G0007/
ATT&CKT1564.003 T1102 T1567 T1218
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where FileName =~ "msedge.exe"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("cmd.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe")
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where ProcessCommandLine has_any ("--window-position=10000,10000","--window-size=1,1","--headless=new","--ignore-certificate-errors")
   or ProcessCommandLine has "webhook.site"
| where ProcessCommandLine has "webhook.site"
   or InitiatingProcessFolderPath has_any (@"\Users\", @"\AppData\", @"\Downloads\")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName,
          Parent = InitiatingProcessFileName,
          ParentCmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          ParentPath = InitiatingProcessFolderPath,
          ChildCmd = ProcessCommandLine,
          SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where NewProcessName endswith "msedge.exe"
| where ParentProcessName endswith "cmd.exe" or ParentProcessName endswith "wscript.exe" or ParentProcessName endswith "cscript.exe"
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where CommandLine has_any ("--window-position=10000,10000","--window-size=1,1","--headless=new","--ignore-certificate-errors")
   or CommandLine has "webhook.site"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName, NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

APT28 MacroMaze: schtasks creating wscript-launched persistence with 20/30/61-minute repeat AIInstallation High
Catches the MacroMaze persistence routine where the CMD dropper writes a Task XML to disk and imports it via schtasks.exe to run a wscript.exe wrapper from %USERPROFILE% taking a .bat as argument. The article's two batch variants set repetition intervals of PT20M, PT30M and PT61M — atypical scheduled-task cadences that combined with the wscript+bat-arg shape are high-fidelity. Rationale: Anchored on the article's specific persistence chain: schtasks /create /xml registering a wscript.exe wrapper that takes a .bat/.cmd argument located in %USERPROFILE%. The PT20M/PT30M/PT61M repetition intervals are exact strings from the campaign and surface as a bonus enrichment, while the structural wscript+bat-arg shape carries the alert. Cross-checked against: • https://thehackernews.com/2026/02/apt28-targeted-european-entities-using.html • https://socprime.com/active-threats/operation-macromaze/ • https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1053/005/
ATT&CKT1053.005 T1059.005 T1059.003
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
// Direct schtasks creation observed by MacroMaze CMD
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(14d)
| where FileName =~ "schtasks.exe"
| where ProcessCommandLine has_any ("/create","/xml")
| where ProcessCommandLine has "wscript"
| where ProcessCommandLine has_any (@"\Users\", "%USERPROFILE%", @"\AppData\")
| where ProcessCommandLine matches regex @"(?i)\.(bat|cmd)\b"
| extend SuspiciousInterval = iff(ProcessCommandLine has_any ("PT20M","PT30M","PT61M","/RI 20","/RI 30","/RI 61"), true, false)
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName,
          Parent = InitiatingProcessFileName,
          ParentCmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          ChildCmd = ProcessCommandLine,
          SuspiciousInterval
| order by Timestamp desc
// SecurityEvent 4688 + 4698 — process creation + task XML registration
(SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(14d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where NewProcessName endswith "schtasks.exe"
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where CommandLine has_any ("/create","/xml")
| where CommandLine has "wscript"
| where CommandLine matches regex @"(?i)\.(bat|cmd)\b"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName, NewProcessName, CommandLine, Source="4688")
| union (
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(14d)
| where EventID == 4698                       // a scheduled task was created
| where EventData has "wscript"
| where EventData matches regex @"(?i)PT(20|30|61)M"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account = SubjectUserName, EventData, Source="4698")
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: APT28 MacroMaze Persistence via schtasks Importing wscript Wrapper
id: a14f77a9-2d18-4c6e-9c2b-0f6e0cf3a7c2
description: Detects schtasks.exe being invoked with /create or /xml to register a task whose action is wscript.exe running a .bat/.cmd argument from a user-profile path — the persistence shape used by APT28's Operation MacroMaze (S2 Grupo Lab52, Jan 2026). The article specifies repetition intervals of 20, 30 and 61 minutes; this rule fires on the wscript+bat structural pattern even before checking the interval.
references:
  - https://lab52.io/blog/operation-macromaze-new-apt28-campaign-using-basic-tooling-and-legit-infrastructure/

  category: process_creation
  product: windows

  selection_proc:
    Image|endswith: '\schtasks.exe'
  selection_create:
    CommandLine|contains:
      - '/create'
      - '/xml'
  selection_wscript:
    CommandLine|contains: 'wscript'
  selection_userprofile:
    CommandLine|contains:
      - '\Users\'
      - '%USERPROFILE%'
      - '\AppData\'
  selection_batarg:
    CommandLine|contains:
      - '.bat'
      - '.cmd'
  filter_machine:
    User|endswith: '$'

APT28 MacroMaze: Office or Edge HTTP traffic to webhook.site (INCLUDEPICTURE tracker + exfil) AICommand & Control Medium
Hunts every leg of the MacroMaze webhook.site abuse: WINWORD.EXE fetching docopened.jpg via the INCLUDEPICTURE tracker, and msedge.exe POSTing the auto-submitted exfil HTML. Either is anomalous in a corporate environment because webhook.site is an ephemeral developer-debugging service that has no business consuming Office or Edge traffic from end-user hosts. Rationale: webhook.site is the article's pivot point — used both for the INCLUDEPICTURE "document opened" beacon (docopened.jpg) and for the auto-submitting HTML exfil POST. Pinning the source process to Office, script hosts or Edge avoids dev-team false positives. Sigma omitted because the rule is a multi-process / cross-table union that doesn't fit a single selection block. Cross-checked against: • https://thehackernews.com/2026/02/apt28-targeted-european-entities-using.html • https://securityaffairs.com/188421/apt/operation-macromaze-apt28-exploits-webhooks-for-covert-data-exfiltration.html • https://socprime.com/active-threats/operation-macromaze/ • https://attack.mitre.org/groups/G0007/
ATT&CKT1102 T1567.002 T1566.001 T1221
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic Web
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(14d)
| where RemoteUrl has "webhook.site" or RemoteUrl endswith ".webhook.site"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ (
    "winword.exe","excel.exe","powerpnt.exe",
    "msedge.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","cmd.exe")
| extend Indicator = case(
    RemoteUrl has "docopened.jpg", "INCLUDEPICTURE_tracker",
    InitiatingProcessFileName =~ "winword.exe", "Office_to_webhook.site",
    InitiatingProcessFileName =~ "msedge.exe", "Edge_exfil_or_payload",
    "Script_host_to_webhook.site")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName,
          Account = InitiatingProcessAccountName,
          Process = InitiatingProcessFileName,
          ProcessCmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          RemoteIP, RemotePort, RemoteUrl, Indicator
| order by Timestamp desc
// Prefer ASIM ImWebSession; fall back to CommonSecurityLog for proxy logs
let webhook_hits = ImWebSession
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(14d)
    | where Url has "webhook.site" or HttpHostName has "webhook.site"
    | where ActingProcessName has_any (
        "winword.exe","excel.exe","powerpnt.exe",
        "msedge.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","cmd.exe")
    | extend Indicator = case(
        Url has "docopened.jpg", "INCLUDEPICTURE_tracker",
        ActingProcessName has "winword", "Office_to_webhook.site",
        ActingProcessName has "msedge", "Edge_exfil_or_payload",
        "Script_host_to_webhook.site")
    | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, HttpMethod, Url, Indicator;
let proxy_hits = CommonSecurityLog
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(14d)
    | where DestinationHostName has "webhook.site" or RequestURL has "webhook.site"
    | extend Indicator = iff(RequestURL has "docopened.jpg", "INCLUDEPICTURE_tracker", "webhook.site_traffic")
    | project TimeGenerated, Computer=SourceHostName, ActorUsername=SourceUserName,
              ActingProcessName=tostring(SourceProcessName),
              HttpMethod=RequestMethod, Url=RequestURL, Indicator;
union isfuzzy=true webhook_hits, proxy_hits
| order by TimeGenerated desc

ATT&CKT1071.001 T1071.004
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic DeviceNetworkEvents
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where RemoteIPType == "Public" and ActionType == "ConnectionSuccess"
| project DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort, Timestamp
| sort by DeviceName asc, RemoteIP asc, RemotePort asc, Timestamp asc
| extend prev_dev = prev(DeviceName, 1), prev_ip = prev(RemoteIP, 1),
         prev_port = prev(RemotePort, 1), prev_ts = prev(Timestamp, 1)
| where DeviceName == prev_dev and RemoteIP == prev_ip and RemotePort == prev_port
| extend delta_sec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, prev_ts)
| summarize conn_count = count(), avg_delta = avg(delta_sec), stdev_delta = stdev(delta_sec)
    by DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| where conn_count > 30 and avg_delta between (30.0 .. 600.0) and stdev_delta < 5.0
| order by conn_count desc
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(2h)
| where DvcAction != "Block"
| where ipv4_is_private(DstIpAddr) == false
| summarize ConnCount = count(),
            DistinctMinutes = dcount(bin(TimeGenerated, 1m))
            by DvcHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber
| where ConnCount > 50 and DistinctMinutes > 30   // sustained, not bursty
| order by ConnCount desc

| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count

title: Infostealer - Non-Browser Process Accessing Browser Credential Stores
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d01
status: experimental
description: |
    Stealers (RedLine, Lumma, Vidar, Atomic) read Login Data / cookies
    SQLite databases from Chrome/Edge/Firefox profile directories. Any
    non-browser process touching these files is a high-fidelity signal.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1539/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1555/003/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1539
    - attack.t1555.003
    - attack.credential-access
logsource:
    category: file_access
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_path:
        TargetFilename|contains:
            - '\Google\Chrome\User Data\'
            - '\Microsoft\Edge\User Data\'
            - '\Mozilla\Firefox\Profiles\'
    selection_file:
        TargetFilename|endswith:
            - '\Login Data'
            - '\Cookies'
            - '\logins.json'
            - '\cookies.sqlite'
    filter_browser:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\chrome.exe'
            - '\msedge.exe'
            - '\firefox.exe'
            - '\brave.exe'
            - '\opera.exe'
    condition: selection_path and selection_file and not filter_browser
falsepositives:
    - Backup utilities reading user profile files
    - Browser sync agents from non-stock vendors
level: high

        OR Filesystem.file_path="*\Mozilla\Firefox\Profiles\*\logins.json*"

Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc
WEB
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time

| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats summariesonly=false count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd

              AttachmentFileName = FileName, AttachmentSHA256 = SHA256;

// Sentinel doesn't have an attachment-hash table. Best approximation:
// OfficeActivity Send/Receive activity + correlate to host process spawn.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let MailWithAttach = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation in~ ("Send","Receive")
    | where isnotempty(MessageId)
    | project EmailTime = TimeGenerated, MailboxOwnerUPN, Sender,
              Recipients, Subject, MessageId;
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName endswith "OUTLOOK.EXE"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName, CommandLine,
          ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Processes DeviceFileEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Pre-filter each table BEFORE the union — the engine cannot push
// predicates across `union`, and DeviceFileEvents lacks ProcessCommandLine
// (Initiating only) so column-projection has to differ per side.

        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"

        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceFileEvents" ),
    ( DeviceProcessEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where AccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("b0f9f0a34ccab1337fbcca24b4f894de8d6d3a6f5db2e0463e2320215e4262e4", "c3b617e0c6b8f01cf628a2b3db40e8d06ef20a3c71365ccc1799787119246010", "df60fa6008b1a0b79c394b42d3ada6bab18b798f3c2ca1530a3e0cb4fbbbe9f6", "58cfb8b9fee1caa94813c259901dc1baa96bae7d30d79b79a7d441d0ee4e577e") or SHA1 in~ ("b0f9f0a34ccab1337fbcca24b4f894de8d6d3a6f5db2e0463e2320215e4262e4", "c3b617e0c6b8f01cf628a2b3db40e8d06ef20a3c71365ccc1799787119246010", "df60fa6008b1a0b79c394b42d3ada6bab18b798f3c2ca1530a3e0cb4fbbbe9f6", "58cfb8b9fee1caa94813c259901dc1baa96bae7d30d79b79a7d441d0ee4e577e") or MD5 in~ ("b0f9f0a34ccab1337fbcca24b4f894de8d6d3a6f5db2e0463e2320215e4262e4", "c3b617e0c6b8f01cf628a2b3db40e8d06ef20a3c71365ccc1799787119246010", "df60fa6008b1a0b79c394b42d3ada6bab18b798f3c2ca1530a3e0cb4fbbbe9f6", "58cfb8b9fee1caa94813c259901dc1baa96bae7d30d79b79a7d441d0ee4e577e")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = ProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceProcessEvents" )
| order by Timestamp desc

// Pre-filter both ASIM tables before the union — same pattern as the
// Defender version so the engine can push hash predicates per-side.
let BadHashes = dynamic(["b0f9f0a34ccab1337fbcca24b4f894de8d6d3a6f5db2e0463e2320215e4262e4", "c3b617e0c6b8f01cf628a2b3db40e8d06ef20a3c71365ccc1799787119246010", "df60fa6008b1a0b79c394b42d3ada6bab18b798f3c2ca1530a3e0cb4fbbbe9f6", "58cfb8b9fee1caa94813c259901dc1baa96bae7d30d79b79a7d441d0ee4e577e"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

CISA KEV: CVE-2026-1731 — BeyondTrust Remote Support (RS) and Privileged Remote Access (PRA) OS Command Injection Vulnerability

CISA KEV
2026-02-13 4 use cases 1 technique 3 kill-chain phases detected

BeyondTrust Remote Support (RS) and Privileged Remote Access (PRA)contain an OS command injection vulnerability. Successful exploitation could allow an unauthenticated remote attacker to execute operating system commands in the context of the site user. Successful exploitation requires no authentication or user interaction and may lead to system compromise, including unauthorized access, data exfiltration, and service disruption. Vendor: BeyondTrust, Product: Remote Support (RS) and Privileged Remote Access (PRA). Known ransomware use: Known. Federal patch due: 2026-02-16.

Actions on Objectives

Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code.
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → Ransomware-style mass file rename / extension change · LSASS process access / dump (credential theft) · Remote service execution — PsExec / SMB lateral movement

Data sourcesVulnerabilities DeviceInfo DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities

| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2026-1731")

| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,

DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities

| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2026-1731")

Ransomware-style mass file rename / extension change Actions on Objectives Medium
Threshold detection: many files renamed in short window, often with new extension.
ATT&CKT1486
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem DeviceFileEvents
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| where ActionType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(FileName) by DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, bin(Timestamp, 1m)
| where files > 200    // empirical: > 200 unique-file renames in 1m by one account on one host
                       //            is well above the P99 of legitimate bulk-tooling
| order by files desc

ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| where EventType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(TargetFileName)
            by DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, bin(TimeGenerated, 1m)
| where files > 200    // P99 of legit bulk-tooling per-minute rename volume
| order by files desc
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.

    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("modified","renamed")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where files > 200
| sort - files
| tstats summariesonly=false count, dc(Filesystem.file_name) AS files
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("modified","renamed")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m

LSASS process access / dump (credential theft) Actions on Objectives High
Mimikatz, comsvcs.dll MiniDump, or any non-Windows process opening LSASS.
ATT&CKT1003.001 T1003
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceEvents
DeviceEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where ActionType == "OpenProcessApiCall"
| where FileName =~ "lsass.exe"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("MsSense.exe","MsMpEng.exe","csrss.exe",
                                          "svchost.exe","wininit.exe","services.exe",
                                          "lsm.exe","SearchProtocolHost.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFolderPath, AccountName
| order by Timestamp desc
// SecurityEvent EventID 4663 = object access. AccessMask 0x10 / 0x40 / 0x1010
// indicate read access to lsass.exe — credential-dump signature. Audit
// Object Access > Audit File System policy required.
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4663
| where ObjectName endswith "\lsass.exe"
| where Account !endswith "$"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ObjectName, ProcessName,
          AccessMask, AccessReason
| order by TimeGenerated desc

level: high

Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents

| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by Timestamp desc

| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)

| where Account !endswith "$"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\psexec.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\psexesvc.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\paexec.exe"
   or (NewProcessName endswith "\wmic.exe" and CommandLine has "/node:")
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName, CommandLine,
          ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Naming and shaming: How ransomware groups tighten the screws on victims

ESET WeLiveSecurity
2026-02-12 8 use cases 3 techniques 4 kill-chain phases detected

When corporate data is exposed on a dedicated leak site, the consequences linger long after the attack fades from the news cycle

ATT&CKT1190 T1486 T1566

Phase 7
Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution · Email attachment opened from external sender
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Office app spawning script/LOLBin child process · Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → RMM tool installed by non-IT user — remote-access utility for hands-on-keyboard
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → Remote service execution — PsExec / SMB lateral movement · Ransomware-style mass file rename / extension change · LSASS process access / dump (credential theft)
Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc
WEB
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time

| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats summariesonly=false count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"

     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]

    [| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes

                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")

                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]

Email attachment opened from external sender Delivery High
Attachment delivery + execution by recipient — most common malware initial access.
ATT&CKT1566.001 T1204.002
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Endpoint.Processes EmailAttachmentInfo DeviceProcessEvents
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let MalAttachments = EmailAttachmentInfo
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | project NetworkMessageId, RecipientEmailAddress,
              AttachmentFileName = FileName, AttachmentSHA256 = SHA256;
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("OUTLOOK.EXE","winword.exe","excel.exe","powerpnt.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("cmd.exe","powershell.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                      "mshta.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe")
| join kind=inner MalAttachments on $left.AccountUpn == $right.RecipientEmailAddress
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, AttachmentFileName, AttachmentSHA256
// Sentinel doesn't have an attachment-hash table. Best approximation:
// OfficeActivity Send/Receive activity + correlate to host process spawn.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let MailWithAttach = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation in~ ("Send","Receive")
    | where isnotempty(MessageId)
    | project EmailTime = TimeGenerated, MailboxOwnerUPN, Sender,
              Recipients, Subject, MessageId;
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName endswith "OUTLOOK.EXE"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName, CommandLine,
          ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

            - '\visio.exe'
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
    condition: selection_parent and selection_child
falsepositives:
    - Legitimate automation macros in trusted internal documents
level: high
ENDPOINT

Ransomware-style mass file rename / extension change Actions on Objectives Medium
Threshold detection: many files renamed in short window, often with new extension.
ATT&CKT1486
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem DeviceFileEvents
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| where ActionType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(FileName) by DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, bin(Timestamp, 1m)
| where files > 200    // empirical: > 200 unique-file renames in 1m by one account on one host
                       //            is well above the P99 of legitimate bulk-tooling
| order by files desc

ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| where EventType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(TargetFileName)
            by DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, bin(TimeGenerated, 1m)
| where files > 200    // P99 of legit bulk-tooling per-minute rename volume
| order by files desc
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count, dc(Filesystem.file_name) AS files
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("modified","renamed")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where files > 200
| sort - files
| tstats summariesonly=false count, dc(Filesystem.file_name) AS files
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("modified","renamed")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where files > 200
| sort - files
LSASS process access / dump (credential theft) Actions on Objectives High
Mimikatz, comsvcs.dll MiniDump, or any non-Windows process opening LSASS.
ATT&CKT1003.001 T1003
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceEvents
DeviceEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where ActionType == "OpenProcessApiCall"
| where FileName =~ "lsass.exe"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("MsSense.exe","MsMpEng.exe","csrss.exe",
                                          "svchost.exe","wininit.exe","services.exe",
                                          "lsm.exe","SearchProtocolHost.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFolderPath, AccountName
| order by Timestamp desc
// SecurityEvent EventID 4663 = object access. AccessMask 0x10 / 0x40 / 0x1010
// indicate read access to lsass.exe — credential-dump signature. Audit
// Object Access > Audit File System policy required.
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4663
| where ObjectName endswith "\lsass.exe"
| where Account !endswith "$"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ObjectName, ProcessName,
          AccessMask, AccessReason
| order by TimeGenerated desc

The Future of AI Agent Security Is Guardrails

Snyk
2026-02-12 1 use case 2 techniques 4 kill-chain phases detected

AI agents introduce new security risks like prompt injection and data exfiltration. Learn how guardrails, hook-based controls, and Arcade’s Contextual Access secure AI agent tool calls in real time.

ATT&CKT1190 T1528
Click any ATT&CK pill below to open it on the Matrix

Exploitation

Installation

Command & Control
Likely
Phase 7
Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code.
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → OAuth consent / suspicious app grant
OAuth consent / suspicious app grant Actions on Objectives High
Cloud identity abuse: app gets high-priv scopes, often via consent phishing.
ATT&CKT1528 T1098.001
Data sourcesAuthentication.Authentication CloudAppEvents
CloudAppEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where ActionType in ("Consent to application.","Add OAuth2PermissionGrant.","Add delegated permission grant.")
| project Timestamp, AccountObjectId, AccountDisplayName, ActivityType,
          ActivityObjects, IPAddress, UserAgent
// Entra ID directory audit — user / admin consent to a third-party app.
AuditLogs
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where OperationName has "Consent to application"
| where Result =~ "success"
| extend AppName = tostring(parse_json(tostring(TargetResources))[0].displayName),
         AppId   = tostring(parse_json(tostring(TargetResources))[0].id),
         User    = tostring(parse_json(tostring(InitiatedBy)).user.userPrincipalName)
| project TimeGenerated, OperationName, User, AppName, AppId, Result
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Exploitability Isn’t the Answer. Breakability Is.

Snyk
2026-02-12 2 use cases 2 techniques 2 kill-chain phases detected

AppSec is often stalled by a lack of trust: will this fix break my app? Snyk’s new Breakability Risk feature solves this by identifying which security updates are safe to merge and which require caution. By focusing on low-risk fixes first, developers can clear backlogs four times faster and reduce security debt without increasing their workload.

author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1195
    - attack.initial-access
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\wmic.exe'
            - '\bitsadmin.exe'
    selection_parent_vendor:
        ParentImage|contains:
            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'
    condition: selection_child and selection_parent_vendor
falsepositives:
    - Many — installers and updaters legitimately spawn children. Pin
      the parent path to specific vendor binaries observed in the
      article before alerting.
level: medium
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`

StepSecurity Detects Early Supply Chain Risk Signals in kilocode npm

StepSecurity
2026-02-11 4 use cases 1 technique 5 kill-chain phases detected

StepSecurity detected early supply chain risk signals in a legitimate kilocode npm release, showing how small behavior changes can quietly weaken trust before attacks happen

ATT&CKT1195.002
Click any ATT&CK pill below to open it on the Matrix
Phase 1
Reconnaissance
—
Phase 2
Weaponization
—
Phase 3
Delivery
Detected
Phase 4
Exploitation
Detected
Phase 5
Installation
Detected
Phase 6
Command & Control
Detected
Phase 7
Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → [LLM] Inventory: @kilocode/cli v1.0.0-v1.0.3 affected-release install on dev workstations
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] npm postinstall: @kilocode/cli platform-binary directory (cli-{platform}-{arch}) write
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → [LLM] npm/yarn/pnpm postinstall: Node child egressing to non-registry public host
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Inventory: @kilocode/cli v1.0.0-v1.0.3 affected-release install on dev workstations AIDelivery High
Identifies developer workstations that installed @kilocode/cli versions v1.0.0 through v1.0.3 — the releases StepSecurity flagged on 2026-01-29 for missing npm provenance attestations and a newly introduced postinstall script. Pure inventory hunt: package is benign but the affected window represents a weakened trust posture that should be tracked and re-installed once provenance is restored. Rationale: Uses the exact package name (@kilocode/cli) and node_modules path convention confirmed in the article body and GitHub issue #5547. Affected version range (v1.0.0-v1.0.3) corroborated by the maintainer thread on GitHub. Tier=hunting because the package itself is benign — this is inventory, not detection of malicious code. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/Kilo-Org/kilocode/issues/5547 • https://www.npmjs.com/package/@kilocode/cli
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1059.007
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Developers legitimately using Kilo Code CLI for AI-assisted coding (treat as inventory, not alert)
  • CI/CD pipelines running `npm install @kilocode/cli` for testing or evaluation
  • Workstation imaging that pre-installs kilocode for AI engineering teams
  • Engineers running the kilocode binary directly after a clean install (post-install execution path looks similar)
  • Cached npm install replays during `npm ci` from package-lock
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessCommandLine !has "ci" or InitiatingProcessParentFileName !in~ ("agent.exe","runner.exe","actions-runner.exe")
Response runbook
1) Triage: Confirm the version installed by reading the node_modules/@kilocode/cli/package.json on the host or via npm ls @kilocode/cli. If version is outside 1.0.0-1.0.3, close as expected. If inside, proceed. 2) Contain: No immediate isolation needed — package is benign per maintainer fix. Instruct user to `npm install @kilocode/cli@latest` to upgrade to a provenance-attested release, and clear any cached older version (`npm cache clean --force`). 3) Hunt: Pivot on same host for any unexpected outbound connections from node.exe during the install window (DeviceNetworkEvents joined by Timestamp ±5min); also check for new entries under @kilocode/cli-darwin-arm64, @kilocode/cli-linux-x64, @kilocode/cli-win32-x64 via UC2.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(60d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("node.exe","node","npm.cmd","npm.exe","yarn.cmd","pnpm.cmd") or FileName in~ ("node.exe","node")
| where ProcessCommandLine has "@kilocode/cli" 
    or InitiatingProcessCommandLine has "@kilocode/cli"
    or FolderPath has @"\node_modules\@kilocode\cli"
    or InitiatingProcessFolderPath has @"\node_modules\@kilocode\cli"
    or ProcessCommandLine has "node_modules/@kilocode/cli"
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName,
          ParentImage = InitiatingProcessFolderPath,
          ParentCmd   = InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          ChildImage  = FolderPath,
          ChildCmd    = ProcessCommandLine,
          SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(60d)
| where ActingProcessName has_any ("node.exe","node","npm.cmd","npm.exe","yarn.cmd","pnpm.cmd")
   or ParentProcessName has_any ("npm.cmd","npm.exe","node.exe","yarn.cmd","pnpm.cmd")
| where ActingProcessCommandLine has "@kilocode/cli"
    or ParentProcessCommandLine has "@kilocode/cli"
    or ActingProcessCommandLine has "node_modules/@kilocode/cli"
    or ActingProcessCommandLine has @"node_modules\@kilocode\cli"
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername,
          ParentCmd = ParentProcessCommandLine,
          ChildCmd  = ActingProcessCommandLine,
          SHA256
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: Affected @kilocode/cli npm Package Install (v1.0.0-v1.0.3 Provenance-Loss Window)
id: 7c2f3ab0-5e8d-4a91-9c4e-2d1a4f6b8c01
description: |
  Detects execution of Node.js or npm/yarn/pnpm tooling against the @kilocode/cli
  package path. The Jan 2026 release moved repositories, lost provenance
  attestations, and introduced a postinstall script that creates unsigned
  platform-binary symlinks. Affected versions 1.0.0-1.0.3.
references:
  - https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/stepsecurity-detects-early-supply-chain-risk-signals-in-kilocode-npm
  - https://github.com/Kilo-Org/kilocode/issues/5547
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/02/09
tags:
  - attack.initial_access
  - attack.t1195.002
  - attack.execution
  - attack.t1059.007
logsource:
  category: process_creation
  product: windows
detection:
  selection_parent:
    ParentImage|endswith:
      - '\node.exe'
      - '\npm.cmd'
      - '\npm.exe'
      - '\yarn.cmd'
      - '\pnpm.cmd'
  selection_path:
    CommandLine|contains:
      - '@kilocode/cli'
      - 'node_modules\@kilocode\cli'
      - 'node_modules/@kilocode/cli'
  condition: selection_parent and selection_path
falsepositives:
  - Internal developers legitimately installing or running Kilo Code CLI (expected — this is an inventory hunt, not a malicious-activity alert)
level: low

npm postinstall: @kilocode/cli platform-binary directory (cli-{platform}-{arch}) write AIInstallation Medium
Hunts for the file-system signature of the postinstall script flagged in the article — creation of @kilocode/cli-darwin-arm64 (and sibling @kilocode/cli-linux-x64 / @kilocode/cli-win32-x64 / @kilocode/cli-darwin-x64) directories under node_modules, written by node.exe spawned from npm/yarn/pnpm. These platform-specific binary packages bypassed integrity verification (no checksum/signature). Pattern generalizes to the broader Shai-Hulud-class supply-chain risk where postinstall scripts drop unsigned native binaries. Rationale: Pins on the exact platform-binary naming pattern (@kilocode/cli-darwin-arm64 named in article, with sibling platform/arch variants inferred from standard npm platform-package convention). Requires the writer to be node/npm tooling, which collapses FPs from unrelated file ops. Tier=hunting because the goal is to surface the install-time behavior for review, not block a known-malicious chain. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/Kilo-Org/kilocode/issues/5547
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1554
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate kilocode install on macOS dev laptop (expected on Apple Silicon Mac fleets)
  • CI runner installing kilocode for AI-coding-related test workflows
  • `npm ci` from a lockfile that still references the affected version range
  • Repository clones where node_modules was committed to the tree
  • Recovery of node_modules from a snapshot/backup process
Response runbook
1) Triage: Stat the @kilocode/cli/package.json on the host to confirm version. If 1.0.4+ (post-fix), close — provenance has been restored. If 1.0.0-1.0.3, validate the platform binary by computing its SHA256 and comparing against the binary now hosted in the post-fix release on npm. 2) Contain: No isolation needed for confirmed-benign installs. Force-upgrade via `npm install @kilocode/cli@latest --force` and `npm cache clean --force`. Add @kilocode/cli<1.0.4 to internal vulnerability tracker as 'low — provenance gap'. 3) Hunt: Pull DeviceNetworkEvents for same DeviceId ±10min around the file event — confirm node.exe only talked to registry.npmjs.org during install (UC3 covers the generalization).

    or FolderPath matches regex @"(?i)/node_modules/@kilocode/cli-(darwin|linux|win32|win64)-(arm64|x64|x86|ia32)/"

          FolderPath, FileName, FileSize,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessParentFileName, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc

npm/yarn/pnpm postinstall: Node child egressing to non-registry public host AICommand & Control Medium
Generalizes the article's core warning ('binaries fetched during installation without integrity verification') and the Shai-Hulud TTP (postinstall/preinstall hook pulls second-stage payload from an attacker-controlled URL). Hunts for node.exe spawned by an npm/yarn/pnpm parent process making outbound public connections to anything other than the canonical npm/yarn/registry/CDN hosts. Designed to catch arbitrary supply-chain postinstall fetches before they become known-bad, not @kilocode/cli specifically. Rationale: Operationalizes the article's core risk thesis ('binaries fetched or linked during installation' without checksum verification) plus the Shai-Hulud preinstall/postinstall second-stage-fetch pattern documented by Microsoft and Datadog. The allowlist (npm/yarn/github/jsdelivr/unpkg) reflects the legitimate package-distribution surface; anything outside it during an npm install is the SOC pivot point. Cross-checked against: • https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2025/12/09/shai-hulud-2-0-guidance-for-detecting-investigating-and-defending-against-the-supply-chain-attack/ • https://securitylabs.datadoghq.com/articles/shai-hulud-2.0-npm-worm/ • https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/monitoring-npm-supply-chain-attacks/
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1105 T1059.007
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate platform-binary fetches from vendor CDNs not in allowlist (e.g. esbuild releases from registry.npmjs.org are covered, but a custom prebuilt-binary host like nodejs.bytecode.dev is not)
  • Cypress, Playwright, Puppeteer, electron-rebuild downloading browser/runtime binaries during install (cypress.io, playwright.azureedge.net, electronjs.org, googleapis.com)
  • Corporate npm registry proxies (Artifactory, Nexus, Verdaccio) on internal hostnames — add to allowlist
  • Telemetry pings from CLI tools sending install-time analytics
  • Devs running `npm test` against APIs from a project root (parent will be shell, not npm — covered by the parent-process filter, but worth noting)
Suppression filter
| where not(RemoteUrl has_any ("cypress.io","download.cypress.io","playwright.azureedge.net","playwright.download.prss.microsoft.com","electronjs.org","github-releases.githubusercontent.com","nodejs.org","chromedriver.storage.googleapis.com","selenium-release.storage.googleapis.com"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: Pull the exact node.exe command line and the parent npm/yarn/pnpm invocation. Identify which package's postinstall fired by reading package-lock.json for the project. Resolve the unknown remote host — WHOIS, abuse.ch ThreatFox, VirusTotal. 2) Contain: If the remote is a known-malicious or fresh domain, network-isolate the workstation, revoke any GitHub/npm/cloud credentials accessible from that user account (Shai-Hulud 2.0 specifically targets these per Microsoft 2025-12-09 guidance), and rotate the user's npm token. 3) Hunt: Pull DeviceFileEvents for the same DeviceId ±15min — look for new files under ~/.npmrc, ~/.config/Code/User/, ~/.claude/, ~/.aws/, ~/.config/gh/, indicating the Shai-Hulud credential-theft + AI-agent-config persistence chain. Search EDR fleet-wide for the same package name in package-lock.json blobs.

Another npm Supply Chain Attack: The 'is' Package Compromise

StepSecurity
2026-02-11 7 use cases 3 techniques 5 kill-chain phases detected

npm 'is' package versions 3.3.1 and 5.0.0 compromised - critical utility with millions of weekly downloads falls victim to expanding phishing campaign

ATT&CKT1190 T1195.002 T1566

Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → [LLM] Scavenger loader/install.js dropped into node_modules (known SHA256 or filename match) · Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution · Email attachment opened from external sender
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Office app spawning script/LOLBin child process · Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] Scavenger npm supply chain: rundll32 executing node-gyp.dll from node_modules (CVE-2025-54313)
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → [LLM] Scavenger npm malware C2 beacon to firebase.su / dieorsuffer.com / smartscreen-api.com
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Scavenger npm supply chain: rundll32 executing node-gyp.dll from node_modules (CVE-2025-54313) AIInstallation High
Detects the Scavenger loader execution chain triggered by the malicious 'is' (3.3.1/5.0.0) and eslint-config-prettier (8.10.1/9.1.1/10.1.6/10.1.7) npm packages. The compromised packages drop a Windows PE32+ DLL named node-gyp.dll into node_modules and invoke it via rundll32.exe from install.js during npm install — a behaviour that is anomalous for a Node.js build dependency. Rationale: The article names malicious versions 3.3.1 and 5.0.0 of 'is' but provides no payload IOCs; cross-referencing Invoke-RE, Snyk, Socket, and Endor Labs identifies the install.js→rundll32→node-gyp.dll chain as the unique execution signature. Rundll32 loading a DLL with that name from a node_modules tree is nearly impossible benignly because node-gyp is a JS build tool, not a DLL provider. Cross-checked against: • https://invokere.com/posts/2025/07/scavenger-malware-distributed-via-eslint-config-prettier-npm-package-supply-chain-compromise/ • https://socket.dev/blog/npm-is-package-hijacked-in-expanding-supply-chain-attack • https://zeropath.com/blog/cve-2025-54313-eslint-config-prettier-supply-chain-malware • https://snyk.io/blog/maintainers-of-eslint-prettier-plugin-attacked-via-npm-supply-chain-malware/ • https://www.endorlabs.com/learn/cve-2025-54313-eslint-config-prettier-compromise----high-severity-but-windows-only

Scavenger loader/install.js dropped into node_modules (known SHA256 or filename match) AIDelivery Medium

Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc
WEB
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats summariesonly=false count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time

Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → [LLM] tj-actions/changed-files compromise: self-hosted runner egress to nikitastupin memdump gist (CVE-2025-30066)
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] tj-actions/changed-files compromise: malicious commit SHA 0e58ed86... referenced on host (CVE-2025-30066) · File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → [LLM] tj-actions/changed-files compromise: memdump.py secret-exfiltration shell pattern on runner (CVE-2025-30066)
tj-actions/changed-files compromise: self-hosted runner egress to nikitastupin memdump gist (CVE-2025-30066) AIDelivery Medium
Detects when a host (typically a self-hosted GitHub Actions runner) makes an outbound HTTP request to the public gist hosting memdump.py — the secret-extraction script delivered by the retroactively re-tagged tj-actions/changed-files Action. The gist ID 30e525b776c409e03c2d6f328f254965 owned by user nikitastupin is hard-coded into the malicious bash payload and is not referenced by any legitimate workflow. Rationale: The gist URL containing user 'nikitastupin' and ID '30e525b776c409e03c2d6f328f254965' is an article-specific, unique IOC explicitly named in the decoded base64 payload. No legitimate workflow fetches this gist. Single match = positive confirmation a compromised tj-actions/changed-files version executed. Cross-checked against: • https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2025-30066 • https://github.com/tj-actions/changed-files/issues/2463 • https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/tj-actions-github-supply-chain-compromise
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1567.001 T1105
Data sourcesWeb Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where RemoteUrl has "nikitastupin/30e525b776c409e03c2d6f328f254965"
    or (RemoteUrl has "gist.githubusercontent.com" and RemoteUrl has "nikitastupin")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessFolderPath, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessAccountName, RemoteUrl, RemoteIP, RemotePort, Protocol
| order by Timestamp desc
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where Url has "nikitastupin/30e525b776c409e03c2d6f328f254965"
    or (Url has "gist.githubusercontent.com" and Url has "nikitastupin")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, SrcIpAddr, SrcHostname, SrcUsername, DstHostname, DstIpAddr, Url, NetworkApplicationProtocol, EventVendor, EventProduct
| order by TimeGenerated desc

tj-actions/changed-files compromise: memdump.py secret-exfiltration shell pattern on runner (CVE-2025-30066) AIActions on Objectives High
Detects process-create events on Linux GitHub Actions runners whose command line contains the unique strings from the decoded malicious payload: the nikitastupin gist ID, memdump.py, the target process name 'Runner.Worker', the bespoke '"isSecret":true' grep filter used to isolate secrets in dumped memory, or the double `base64 -w 0` encoding used to wrap the dumped secrets for inclusion in build logs. Rationale: All five payload strings appear in the article's decoded bash blob and are unique to this attack: the gist ID identifies the loader, memdump.py the script, Runner.Worker is the GHA agent process the script searches /proc for, '"isSecret":true' is the bespoke grep pattern targeting the secret JSON shape in runner memory, and 'base64 -w 0 | base64 -w 0' is the unusual double-encoding the adversary uses to smuggle the dump through the build log. Cross-checked against: • https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2025-30066 • https://github.com/tj-actions/changed-files/issues/2463 • https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/tj-actions-github-supply-chain-compromise
ATT&CKT1552.001 T1059.004 T1195.002 T1003
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where ProcessCommandLine has_any (
        "nikitastupin/30e525b776c409e03c2d6f328f254965",
        "memdump.py",
        "Runner.Worker",
        @"""isSecret"":true",
        "base64 -w 0 | base64 -w 0"
    )
   or InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any (
        "nikitastupin/30e525b776c409e03c2d6f328f254965",
        "memdump.py",
        @"""isSecret"":true"
    )
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, FolderPath, ProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFolderPath,
          InitiatingProcessParentFileName, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where TargetProcessCommandLine has_any (
        "nikitastupin/30e525b776c409e03c2d6f328f254965",
        "memdump.py",
        "Runner.Worker",
        @"""isSecret"":true",
        "base64 -w 0 | base64 -w 0"
    )
   or ParentProcessCommandLine has_any (
        "nikitastupin/30e525b776c409e03c2d6f328f254965",
        "memdump.py",
        @"""isSecret"":true"
